Related News Over 1,100 host community projects ongoing in Niger Delta, says NUPRC Oil block bidding: Inside Nigeria’s seven-hour transparency test REAN urges action to accelerate Nigeria’s clean energy transition In a published report, the President-General of the Niger Delta Progressive Alliance, Nse Udoh, argued that pipeline surveillance has helped national institutions move from reactive crisis management to more strategic planning and long-term decision-making. “It is important to clarify the role of pipeline surveillance within the wider energy landscape. Energy security encompasses the full value chain, from exploration and production to refining, distribution, pricing policy, and subsidy frameworks. Pipeline surveillance does not manage these domains,” he said. Udoh, however, maintained that pipeline surveillance plays a critical supporting role by protecting infrastructure that transports petroleum resources. “Its mandate is precise: safeguarding critical infrastructure that transports petroleum resources. Yet this single function has proven foundational. Without secure transportation channels, production targets falter, refining plans collapse, exports decline, and fiscal projections become unreliable,” he added. He further argued that asset protection underpins the efficient operation of the petroleum value chain. “Asset protection, in this context, is not a supporting activity. It is a precondition for economic order. In effect, the pipeline is the hinge on which the entire petroleum value chain turns. When that hinge is weak, every other link in the chain carries strain. When it is secure, the entire system gains coherence,” he wrote. According to him, sustained monitoring and rapid-response measures have contributed to fewer pipeline breaches and incidents of illegal tapping, while improvements in production have helped strengthen Nigeria’s position in the international oil market. Udoh also argued that greater certainty in crude oil transportation supports planning across the petroleum value chain. “Economic stability follows predictability. When crude flows are secure, refineries can plan feedstock intake with assurance. Export commitments can be met without fear of sudden shortfalls. Gas-to-power projects can operate without recurrent shutdown risks. “Investors can assess Nigeria’s petroleum sector with clearer risk profiles. Surveillance therefore does more than stop theft. It reintroduces reliability into national energy planning. And reliability is the bedrock upon which sustainable economic growth is built. “With predictable flows, national budgeting becomes more credible, infrastructure planning becomes more precise, and long-term contracts become easier to negotiate. Predictability is the silent currency of modern economies, and pipeline surveillance has begun restoring it,” he stated. He added that improved production accountability has supported export earnings, strengthened foreign exchange inflows and enhanced fiscal capacity, while helping national oil company operations become more efficient through lower losses and improved operational continuity. According to Udoh, more stable revenues also improve government planning by creating greater fiscal certainty and supporting investment in infrastructure, social services and economic diversification. Traditional rulers and community leaders in the Niger Delta have also called for continued support for Tantita Security Services Limited following a vote of confidence passed on the company by the National Assembly. The leaders said the company’s involvement in protecting oil and gas pipelines had coincided with improvements in security across some oil-producing communities, while also creating employment opportunities for young people. Commenting on the development, the President-General of the Isoko Development Union, Christopher Akpotu, commended the joint committee of the Senate and House of Representatives for dismissing petitions filed against the company. He described the decision as beneficial to the Niger Delta and urged stakeholders to focus on expanding opportunities in the oil and gas sector rather than internal disputes. “That is the right way to go,” he said. “There are many opportunities in the oil and gas sector. We should focus on how to derive more benefits rather than fighting over what has already been allocated.” Akpotu cautioned that prolonged disagreements among host communities could create opportunities for outsiders to benefit at their expense. “At the end of the day, if we continue fighting, we give room to those who have no stake in our communities to take what rightfully belongs to us,” he added. While acknowledging concerns about the distribution of benefits, he maintained that the surveillance contract had positively affected many communities. “The truth is that many communities and youths have benefited from the surveillance contract. It may not be evenly distributed, but the impact is evident,” he said. He also urged Tantita to broaden engagement with traditional rulers, youths and community leaders to promote fairness and strengthen long-term cooperation. Similarly, the President-General of the Ughelli Descendants Union, Sam Akpemegi, said security had improved since the company began operations, noting that both physical surveillance and intelligence-driven strategies had been deployed. “They have done very well and improved security since they began operations,” he said, adding that the company’s activities now extend across many communities. Akpemegi added that the company’s presence had benefited both traditional institutions and local residents, while calling for continued support for its operations. In the same vein, the Odiologbo of Ofagbe Kingdom, Ogaga Ikpoku, backed the National Assembly’s decision, describing it as timely and beneficial to peace and stability in the region. Stakeholders also argued that improved protection of oil assets has produced environmental and social benefits in parts of the Niger Delta. They noted that illegal refining and pipeline sabotage had previously polluted waterways, degraded farmlands and exposed communities to environmental and security risks, adding that sustained surveillance efforts against illegal refining sites and repeated pipeline breaches have supported ongoing environmental recovery. The company works alongside other security agencies in carrying out surveillance activities aimed at protecting oil facilities and supporting stability in the Niger Delta. Stakeholders say improved surveillance has contributed to efforts to reduce pipeline vandalism and crude oil theft while supporting more stable production levels. They also argue that protecting oil infrastructure remains critical to sustaining investment, improving production and supporting government revenue. The Chief Executive Officer of the Centre for the Promotion of Private Enterprise, Muda Yusuf, said Nigeria’s energy transition agenda makes it important to secure existing oil assets while expanding production. He described the 2025 licensing round as an effort to strengthen the country’s energy sector but said improved security must accompany investment initiatives. “We are in the age of energy transition. NUPRC’s operation in this exercise is good for the economy. It will boost foreign reserves, firm up the naira and macroeconomic environment while deepening foreign capital inflows to the domestic economy,” he said. Yusuf added that stronger protection of oil and gas infrastructure could encourage additional investment in the sector. “The new momentum generated will help build capacity to increase the level of production in the oil and gas industry,” he added. Abuja-based energy expert Steven Martins said the outcome of the licensing round represented more than a routine administrative exercise, noting that sustaining oil asset protection would be important as new investments move into the operational phase. “It represents a strategic recalibration of Nigeria’s energy ambitions at a time when global markets are shifting rapidly, and the country faces pressure to strengthen production, attract capital, and reposition itself in the era of energy transition. “As NUPRC is looking forward to harvesting an additional 500 million barrels to the national crude oil reserve from the 2025 bid round, these extra barrels are expected to be driven by the commencement of operational activities of the 31 companies that emerged as winners of 37 oil and gas blocks in the bid round licensing. Protection of oil assets should also form part of the priority areas for stakeholders, and that is where TSSNL should always be supported to achieve its goals,” he said. Martins explained that the awarded assets span several producing and frontier basins, including 16 Niger Delta Onshore blocks, 18 Niger Delta Shallow Water blocks, one Niger Delta Deep Offshore block, three Benin Basin Onshore blocks, four Anambra Basin Onshore blocks, four Chad Basin Onshore blocks and four Benue Trough blocks. The Chief Executive Officer, NUPRC, Mrs Oritsemeyiwa Eyesan, said at the Commercial Bid Conference for the Nigeria Licensing Round, themed “Expanding Opportunities: Right Play, Right Place, Right Time”, that the assets on offer have significant reserve potential. “The assets available in this licensing round have potential to add about 500 million barrels to Nigeria’s reserves, increasing our existing reserves of crude oil and condensate—which currently stand at 37.01 billion barrels—and 215.19 trillion cubic feet of gas,” she said. Eyesan said the absence of bids for the 13 frontier blocks was not unexpected. According to her, the assets presented during the 2025 commercial bid had previously been recovered from operators, while the commission would continue assessing other assets for possible inclusion in future licensing exercises. “The reason why we are in the market is because of the blocks that we have recovered from existing operators,” she said. Industry analysts also noted that the licensing round attracted notable investor interest in frontier basins. Some winners The companies that emerged as winners of the 2025 Licensing Round include SSonic Petroleum Limited (PPL 2A29), CFP Pipeline and Flowlines (2A30), Dutchford E&P Limited (2A32), Attabanson Global Company Limited (2A33 and PPL 901), Rosem Energy Limited (2A38), and Pivot-GIS Limited (2A39). The others include Network E&P (2A40), Asharami (2A41), LexOil (2A42), BVOF (2A43), GupscoEnergy Limited (2A44 and 2A51), Saratoga (2A45), Volante (2A46), Concept-Reel Petroleum Services Limited (2A47 and 2A55), Clinton Oil Field (2A48 and 2A62), Nuway Oaklane Limited (2A49), Ramec (2A50), Italia (2A53), Blueridge E&P (2A54), Up Energies Limited (2A56), and AYM Shafa (2A57). There are also Blackrock Holdings Limited (2A58), Funtay Integrated Business Limited (2A59), Riparian Development and Production Limited (2A60), Nikstallis (2A61 and PPL 900), Stardeep Petroleum (PPL 2010), Dakoda & U Limited (PPL 308 and PPL 800), Southborne Oil and Gas Limited (PPL 902), Lanaka Petroleum (PPL 903), Highban Resources Limited (PPL 700) and Eyre Energy Limited (PPL 801). NUPRC’s operation in this exercise is good for the economy. It will boost foreign reserves, firm up the naira and macroeconomic environment while deepening foreign capital inflows to the domestic economy,” he said. Yusuf added that stronger protection of oil and gas infrastructure could encourage additional investment in the sector. “The new momentum generated will help build capacity to increase the level of production in the oil and gas industry,” he added. Abuja-based energy expert Steven Martins said the outcome of the licensing round represented more than a routine administrative exercise, noting that sustaining oil asset protection would be important as new investments move into the operational phase. “It represents a strategic recalibration of Nigeria’s energy ambitions at a time when global markets are shifting rapidly, and the country faces pressure to strengthen production, attract capital, and reposition itself in the era of energy transition. “As NUPRC is looking forward to harvesting an additional 500 million barrels to the national crude oil reserve from the 2025 bid round, these extra barrels are expected to be driven by the commencement of operational activities of the 31 companies that emerged as winners of 37 oil and gas blocks in the bid round licensing. Protection of oil assets should also form part of the priority areas for stakeholders, and that is where TSSNL should always be supported to achieve its goals,” he said. Martins explained that the awarded assets span several producing and frontier basins, including 16 Niger Delta Onshore blocks, 18 Niger Delta Shallow Water blocks, one Niger Delta Deep Offshore block, three Benin Basin Onshore blocks, four Anambra Basin Onshore blocks, four Chad Basin Onshore blocks and four Benue Trough blocks. The Chief Executive Officer, NUPRC, Mrs Oritsemeyiwa Eyesan, said at the Commercial Bid Conference for the Nigeria Licensing Round, themed “Expanding Opportunities: Right Play, Right Place, Right Time”, that the assets on offer have significant reserve potential. “The assets available in this licensing round have potential to add about 500 million barrels to Nigeria’s reserves, increasing our existing reserves of crude oil and condensate—which currently stand at 37.01 billion barrels—and 215.19 trillion cubic feet of gas,” she said. Eyesan said the absence of bids for the 13 frontier blocks was not unexpected. According to her, the assets presented during the 2025 commercial bid had previously been recovered from operators, while the commission would continue assessing other assets for possible inclusion in future licensing exercises. “The reason why we are in the market is because of the blocks that we have recovered from existing operators,” she said. Industry analysts also noted that the licensing round attracted notable investor interest in frontier basins. Some winners The companies that emerged as winners of the 2025 Licensing Round include SSonic Petroleum Limited (PPL 2A29), CFP Pipeline and Flowlines (2A30), Dutchford E&P Limited (2A32), Attabanson Global Company Limited (2A33 and PPL 901), Rosem Energy Limited (2A38), and Pivot-GIS Limited (2A39). The others include Network E&P (2A40), Asharami (2A41), LexOil (2A42), BVOF (2A43), GupscoEnergy Limited (2A44 and 2A51), Saratoga (2A45), Volante (2A46), Concept-Reel Petroleum Services Limited (2A47 and 2A55), Clinton Oil Field (2A48 and 2A62), Nuway Oaklane Limited (2A49), Ramec (2A50), Italia (2A53), Blueridge E&P (2A54), Up Energies Limited (2A56), and AYM Shafa (2A57). There are also Blackrock Holdings Limited (2A58), Funtay Integrated Business Limited (2A59), Riparian Development and Production Limited (2A60), Nikstallis (2A61 and PPL 900), Stardeep Petroleum (PPL 2010), Dakoda & U Limited (PPL 308 and PPL 800), Southborne Oil and Gas Limited (PPL 902), Lanaka Petroleum (PPL 903), Highban Resources Limited (PPL 700) and Eyre Energy Limited (PPL 801). Views from stakeholders Stakeholders have continued to emphasise the importance of pipeline protection to Nigeria’s oil production ambitions, arguing that safeguarding critical infrastructure remains essential to sustaining output, attracting investment and supporting broader economic growth. Related News Over 1,100 host community projects ongoing in Niger Delta, says NUPRC Oil block bidding: Inside Nigeria’s seven-hour transparency test REAN urges action to accelerate Nigeria’s clean energy transition In a published report, the President-General of the Niger Delta Progressive Alliance, Nse Udoh, argued that pipeline surveillance has helped national institutions move from reactive crisis management to more strategic planning and long-term decision-making. “It is important to clarify the role of pipeline surveillance within the wider energy landscape. Energy security encompasses the full value chain, from exploration and production to refining, distribution, pricing policy, and subsidy frameworks. Pipeline surveillance does not manage these domains,” he said. Udoh, however, maintained that pipeline surveillance plays a critical supporting role by protecting infrastructure that transports petroleum resources. “Its mandate is precise: safeguarding critical infrastructure that transports petroleum resources. Yet this single function has proven foundational. Without secure transportation channels, production targets falter, refining plans collapse, exports decline, and fiscal projections become unreliable,” he added. He further argued that asset protection underpins the efficient operation of the petroleum value chain. “Asset protection, in this context, is not a supporting activity. It is a precondition for economic order. In effect, the pipeline is the hinge on which the entire petroleum value chain turns. When that hinge is weak, every other link in the chain carries strain. When it is secure, the entire system gains coherence,” he wrote. According to him, sustained monitoring and rapid-response measures have contributed to fewer pipeline breaches and incidents of illegal tapping, while improvements in production have helped strengthen Nigeria’s position in the international oil market. Udoh also argued that greater certainty in crude oil transportation supports planning across the petroleum value chain. “Economic stability follows predictability. When crude flows are secure, refineries can plan feedstock intake with assurance. Export commitments can be met without fear of sudden shortfalls. Gas-to-power projects can operate without recurrent shutdown risks. “Investors can assess Nigeria’s petroleum sector with clearer risk profiles. Surveillance therefore does more than stop theft. It reintroduces reliability into national energy planning. And reliability is the bedrock upon which sustainable economic growth is built. “With predictable flows, national budgeting becomes more credible, infrastructure planning becomes more precise, and long-term contracts become easier to negotiate. Predictability is the silent currency of modern economies, and pipeline surveillance has begun restoring it,” he stated. He added that improved production accountability has supported export earnings, strengthened foreign exchange inflows and enhanced fiscal capacity, while helping national oil company operations become more efficient through lower losses and improved operational continuity. According to Udoh, more stable revenues also improve government planning by creating greater fiscal certainty and supporting investment in infrastructure, social services and economic diversification. Traditional rulers and community leaders in the Niger Delta have also called for continued support for Tantita Security Services Limited following a vote of confidence passed on the company by the National Assembly. The leaders said the company’s involvement in protecting oil and gas pipelines had coincided with improvements in security across some oil-producing communities, while also creating employment opportunities for young people. Commenting on the development, the President-General of the Isoko Development Union, Christopher Akpotu, commended the joint committee of the Senate and House of Representatives for dismissing petitions filed against the company. He described the decision as beneficial to the Niger Delta and urged stakeholders to focus on expanding opportunities in the oil and gas sector rather than internal disputes. “That is the right way to go,” he said. “There are many opportunities in the oil and gas sector. We should focus on how to derive more benefits rather than fighting over what has already been allocated.” Akpotu cautioned that prolonged disagreements among host communities could create opportunities for outsiders to benefit at their expense. “At the end of the day, if we continue fighting, we give room to those who have no stake in our communities to take what rightfully belongs to us,” he added. While acknowledging concerns about the distribution of benefits, he maintained that the surveillance contract had positively affected many communities. “The truth is that many communities and youths have benefited from the surveillance contract. It may not be evenly distributed, but the impact is evident,” he said. He also urged Tantita to broaden engagement with traditional rulers, youths and community leaders to promote fairness and strengthen long-term cooperation. Similarly, the President-General of the Ughelli Descendants Union, Sam Akpemegi, said security had improved since the company began operations, noting that both physical surveillance and intelligence-driven strategies had been deployed. “They have done very well and improved security since they began operations,” he said, adding that the company’s activities now extend across many communities. Akpemegi added that the company’s presence had benefited both traditional institutions and local residents, while calling for continued support for its operations. In the same vein, the Odiologbo of Ofagbe Kingdom, Ogaga Ikpoku, backed the National Assembly’s decision, describing it as timely and beneficial to peace and stability in the region. Stakeholders also argued that improved protection of oil assets has produced environmental and social benefits in parts of the Niger Delta. They noted that illegal refining and pipeline sabotage had previously polluted waterways, degraded farmlands and exposed communities to environmental and security risks, adding that sustained surveillance efforts against illegal refining sites and repeated pipeline breaches have supported ongoing environmental recovery. “We are in the age of energy transition. NUPRC’s operation in this exercise is good for the economy. It will boost foreign reserves, firm up the naira and macroeconomic environment while deepening foreign capital inflows to the domestic economy,” he said. Yusuf added that stronger protection of oil and gas infrastructure could encourage additional investment in the sector. “The new momentum generated will help build capacity to increase the level of production in the oil and gas industry,” he added. Abuja-based energy expert Steven Martins said the outcome of the licensing round represented more than a routine administrative exercise, noting that sustaining oil asset protection would be important as new investments move into the operational phase. “It represents a strategic recalibration of Nigeria’s energy ambitions at a time when global markets are shifting rapidly, and the country faces pressure to strengthen production, attract capital, and reposition itself in the era of energy transition. “As NUPRC is looking forward to harvesting an additional 500 million barrels to the national crude oil reserve from the 2025 bid round, these extra barrels are expected to be driven by the commencement of operational activities of the 31 companies that emerged as winners of 37 oil and gas blocks in the bid round licensing. Protection of oil assets should also form part of the priority areas for stakeholders, and that is where TSSNL should always be supported to achieve its goals,” he said. Martins explained that the awarded assets span several producing and frontier basins, including 16 Niger Delta Onshore blocks, 18 Niger Delta Shallow Water blocks, one Niger Delta Deep Offshore block, three Benin Basin Onshore blocks, four Anambra Basin Onshore blocks, four Chad Basin Onshore blocks and four Benue Trough blocks. The leaders said the company’s involvement in protecting oil and gas pipelines had coincided with improvements in security across some oil-producing communities, while also creating employment opportunities for young people. Commenting on the development, the President-General of the Isoko Development Union, Christopher Akpotu, commended the joint committee of the Senate and House of Representatives for dismissing petitions filed against the company. He described the decision as beneficial to the Niger Delta and urged stakeholders to focus on expanding opportunities in the oil and gas sector rather than internal disputes. “That is the right way to go,” he said. “There are many opportunities in the oil and gas sector. We should focus on how to derive more benefits rather than fighting over what has already been allocated.” Akpotu cautioned that prolonged disagreements among host communities could create opportunities for outsiders to benefit at their expense. “At the end of the day, if we continue fighting, we give room to those who have no stake in our communities to take what rightfully belongs to us,” he added. While acknowledging concerns about the distribution of benefits, he maintained that the surveillance contract had positively affected many communities. “The truth is that many communities and youths have benefited from the surveillance contract. It may not be evenly distributed, but the impact is evident,” he said. He also urged Tantita to broaden engagement with traditional rulers, youths and community leaders to promote fairness and strengthen long-term cooperation. Similarly, the President-General of the Ughelli Descendants Union, Sam Akpemegi, said security had improved since the company began operations, noting that both physical surveillance and intelligence-driven strategies had been deployed. “They have done very well and improved security since they began operations,” he said, adding that the company’s activities now extend across many communities. Akpemegi added that the company’s presence had benefited both traditional institutions and local residents, while calling for continued support for its operations. In the same vein, the Odiologbo of Ofagbe Kingdom, Ogaga Ikpoku, backed the National Assembly’s decision, describing it as timely and beneficial to peace and stability in the region. Stakeholders also argued that improved protection of oil assets has produced environmental and social benefits in parts of the Niger Delta. They noted that illegal refining and pipeline sabotage had previously polluted waterways, degraded farmlands and exposed communities to environmental and security risks, adding that sustained surveillance efforts against illegal refining sites and repeated pipeline breaches have supported ongoing environmental recovery. “It represents a strategic recalibration of Nigeria’s energy ambitions at a time when global markets are shifting rapidly, and the country faces pressure to strengthen production, attract capital, and reposition itself in the era of energy transition. “As NUPRC is looking forward to harvesting an additional 500 million barrels to the national crude oil reserve from the 2025 bid round, these extra barrels are expected to be driven by the commencement of operational activities of the 31 companies that emerged as winners of 37 oil and gas blocks in the bid round licensing. Protection of oil assets should also form part of the priority areas for stakeholders, and that is where TSSNL should always be supported to achieve its goals,” he said. Martins explained that the awarded assets span several producing and frontier basins, including 16 Niger Delta Onshore blocks, 18 Niger Delta Shallow Water blocks, one Niger Delta Deep Offshore block, three Benin Basin Onshore blocks, four Anambra Basin Onshore blocks, four Chad Basin Onshore blocks and four Benue Trough blocks. The Chief Executive Officer, NUPRC, Mrs Oritsemeyiwa Eyesan, said at the Commercial Bid Conference for the Nigeria Licensing Round, themed “Expanding Opportunities: Right Play, Right Place, Right Time”, that the assets on offer have significant reserve potential. “The assets available in this licensing round have potential to add about 500 million barrels to Nigeria’s reserves, increasing our existing reserves of crude oil and condensate—which currently stand at 37.01 billion barrels—and 215.19 trillion cubic feet of gas,” she said. Eyesan said the absence of bids for the 13 frontier blocks was not unexpected. According to her, the assets presented during the 2025 commercial bid had previously been recovered from operators, while the commission would continue assessing other assets for possible inclusion in future licensing exercises. “The reason why we are in the market is because of the blocks that we have recovered from existing operators,” she said. Industry analysts also noted that the licensing round attracted notable investor interest in frontier basins. Some winners The companies that emerged as winners of the 2025 Licensing Round include SSonic Petroleum Limited (PPL 2A29), CFP Pipeline and Flowlines (2A30), Dutchford E&P Limited (2A32), Attabanson Global Company Limited (2A33 and PPL 901), Rosem Energy Limited (2A38), and Pivot-GIS Limited (2A39). The others include Network E&P (2A40), Asharami (2A41), LexOil (2A42), BVOF (2A43), GupscoEnergy Limited (2A44 and 2A51), Saratoga (2A45), Volante (2A46), Concept-Reel Petroleum Services Limited (2A47 and 2A55), Clinton Oil Field (2A48 and 2A62), Nuway Oaklane Limited (2A49), Ramec (2A50), Italia (2A53), Blueridge E&P (2A54), Up Energies Limited (2A56), and AYM Shafa (2A57). There are also Blackrock Holdings Limited (2A58), Funtay Integrated Business Limited (2A59), Riparian Development and Production Limited (2A60), Nikstallis (2A61 and PPL 900), Stardeep Petroleum (PPL 2010), Dakoda & U Limited (PPL 308 and PPL 800), Southborne Oil and Gas Limited (PPL 902), Lanaka Petroleum (PPL 903), Highban Resources Limited (PPL 700) and Eyre Energy Limited (PPL 801). Views from stakeholders Stakeholders have continued to emphasise the importance of pipeline protection to Nigeria’s oil production ambitions, arguing that safeguarding critical infrastructure remains essential to sustaining output, attracting investment and supporting broader economic growth. Related News Over 1,100 host community projects ongoing in Niger Delta, says NUPRC Oil block bidding: Inside Nigeria’s seven-hour transparency test REAN urges action to accelerate Nigeria’s clean energy transition In a published report, the President-General of the Niger Delta Progressive Alliance, Nse Udoh, argued that pipeline surveillance has helped national institutions move from reactive crisis management to more strategic planning and long-term decision-making. “It is important to clarify the role of pipeline surveillance within the wider energy landscape. Energy security encompasses the full value chain, from exploration and production to refining, distribution, pricing policy, and subsidy frameworks. Pipeline surveillance does not manage these domains,” he said. Udoh, however, maintained that pipeline surveillance plays a critical supporting role by protecting infrastructure that transports petroleum resources. “Its mandate is precise: safeguarding critical infrastructure that transports petroleum resources. Yet this single function has proven foundational. Without secure transportation channels, production targets falter, refining plans collapse, exports decline, and fiscal projections become unreliable,” he added. He further argued that asset protection underpins the efficient operation of the petroleum value chain. “Asset protection, in this context, is not a supporting activity. It is a precondition for economic order. In effect, the pipeline is the hinge on which the entire petroleum value chain turns. When that hinge is weak, every other link in the chain carries strain. When it is secure, the entire system gains coherence,” he wrote. According to him, sustained monitoring and rapid-response measures have contributed to fewer pipeline breaches and incidents of illegal tapping, while improvements in production have helped strengthen Nigeria’s position in the international oil market. Udoh also argued that greater certainty in crude oil transportation supports planning across the petroleum value chain. “Economic stability follows predictability. When crude flows are secure, refineries can plan feedstock intake with assurance. Export commitments can be met without fear of sudden shortfalls. Gas-to-power projects can operate without recurrent shutdown risks. “Investors can assess Nigeria’s petroleum sector with clearer risk profiles. Surveillance therefore does more than stop theft. It reintroduces reliability into national energy planning. And reliability is the bedrock upon which sustainable economic growth is built. “With predictable flows, national budgeting becomes more credible, infrastructure planning becomes more precise, and long-term contracts become easier to negotiate. Predictability is the silent currency of modern economies, and pipeline surveillance has begun restoring it,” he stated. He added that improved production accountability has supported export earnings, strengthened foreign exchange inflows and enhanced fiscal capacity, while helping national oil company operations become more efficient through lower losses and improved operational continuity. According to Udoh, more stable revenues also improve government planning by creating greater fiscal certainty and supporting investment in infrastructure, social services and economic diversification. Similarly, the President-General of the Ughelli Descendants Union, Sam Akpemegi, said security had improved since the company began operations, noting that both physical surveillance and intelligence-driven strategies had been deployed. “They have done very well and improved security since they began operations,” he said, adding that the company’s activities now extend across many communities. Akpemegi added that the company’s presence had benefited both traditional institutions and local residents, while calling for continued support for its operations. In the same vein, the Odiologbo of Ofagbe Kingdom, Ogaga Ikpoku, backed the National Assembly’s decision, describing it as timely and beneficial to peace and stability in the region. Stakeholders also argued that improved protection of oil assets has produced environmental and social benefits in parts of the Niger Delta. They noted that illegal refining and pipeline sabotage had previously polluted waterways, degraded farmlands and exposed communities to environmental and security risks, adding that sustained surveillance efforts against illegal refining sites and repeated pipeline breaches have supported ongoing environmental recovery. “The assets available in this licensing round have potential to add about 500 million barrels to Nigeria’s reserves, increasing our existing reserves of crude oil and condensate—which currently stand at 37.01 billion barrels—and 215.19 trillion cubic feet of gas,” she said. Eyesan said the absence of bids for the 13 frontier blocks was not unexpected. According to her, the assets presented during the 2025 commercial bid had previously been recovered from operators, while the commission would continue assessing other assets for possible inclusion in future licensing exercises. “The reason why we are in the market is because of the blocks that we have recovered from existing operators,” she said. Industry analysts also noted that the licensing round attracted notable investor interest in frontier basins. Some winners The companies that emerged as winners of the 2025 Licensing Round include SSonic Petroleum Limited (PPL 2A29), CFP Pipeline and Flowlines (2A30), Dutchford E&P Limited (2A32), Attabanson Global Company Limited (2A33 and PPL 901), Rosem Energy Limited (2A38), and Pivot-GIS Limited (2A39). The others include Network E&P (2A40), Asharami (2A41), LexOil (2A42), BVOF (2A43), GupscoEnergy Limited (2A44 and 2A51), Saratoga (2A45), Volante (2A46), Concept-Reel Petroleum Services Limited (2A47 and 2A55), Clinton Oil Field (2A48 and 2A62), Nuway Oaklane Limited (2A49), Ramec (2A50), Italia (2A53), Blueridge E&P (2A54), Up Energies Limited (2A56), and AYM Shafa (2A57). There are also Blackrock Holdings Limited (2A58), Funtay Integrated Business Limited (2A59), Riparian Development and Production Limited (2A60), Nikstallis (2A61 and PPL 900), Stardeep Petroleum (PPL 2010), Dakoda & U Limited (PPL 308 and PPL 800), Southborne Oil and Gas Limited (PPL 902), Lanaka Petroleum (PPL 903), Highban Resources Limited (PPL 700) and Eyre Energy Limited (PPL 801). Views from stakeholders Stakeholders have continued to emphasise the importance of pipeline protection to Nigeria’s oil production ambitions, arguing that safeguarding critical infrastructure remains essential to sustaining output, attracting investment and supporting broader economic growth. Related News Over 1,100 host community projects ongoing in Niger Delta, says NUPRC Oil block bidding: Inside Nigeria’s seven-hour transparency test REAN urges action to accelerate Nigeria’s clean energy transition In a published report, the President-General of the Niger Delta Progressive Alliance, Nse Udoh, argued that pipeline surveillance has helped national institutions move from reactive crisis management to more strategic planning and long-term decision-making. “It is important to clarify the role of pipeline surveillance within the wider energy landscape. Energy security encompasses the full value chain, from exploration and production to refining, distribution, pricing policy, and subsidy frameworks. Pipeline surveillance does not manage these domains,” he said. Udoh, however, maintained that pipeline surveillance plays a critical supporting role by protecting infrastructure that transports petroleum resources. “Its mandate is precise: safeguarding critical infrastructure that transports petroleum resources. Yet this single function has proven foundational. Without secure transportation channels, production targets falter, refining plans collapse, exports decline, and fiscal projections become unreliable,” he added. He further argued that asset protection underpins the efficient operation of the petroleum value chain. “Asset protection, in this context, is not a supporting activity. It is a precondition for economic order. In effect, the pipeline is the hinge on which the entire petroleum value chain turns. When that hinge is weak, every other link in the chain carries strain. When it is secure, the entire system gains coherence,” he wrote. According to him, sustained monitoring and rapid-response measures have contributed to fewer pipeline breaches and incidents of illegal tapping, while improvements in production have helped strengthen Nigeria’s position in the international oil market. Udoh also argued that greater certainty in crude oil transportation supports planning across the petroleum value chain. “Economic stability follows predictability. When crude flows are secure, refineries can plan feedstock intake with assurance. Export commitments can be met without fear of sudden shortfalls. Gas-to-power projects can operate without recurrent shutdown risks. “Investors can assess Nigeria’s petroleum sector with clearer risk profiles. Surveillance therefore does more than stop theft. It reintroduces reliability into national energy planning. And reliability is the bedrock upon which sustainable economic growth is built. “With predictable flows, national budgeting becomes more credible, infrastructure planning becomes more precise, and long-term contracts become easier to negotiate. Predictability is the silent currency of modern economies, and pipeline surveillance has begun restoring it,” he stated. He added that improved production accountability has supported export earnings, strengthened foreign exchange inflows and enhanced fiscal capacity, while helping national oil company operations become more efficient through lower losses and improved operational continuity. According to Udoh, more stable revenues also improve government planning by creating greater fiscal certainty and supporting investment in infrastructure, social services and economic diversification. Traditional rulers and community leaders in the Niger Delta have also called for continued support for Tantita Security Services Limited following a vote of confidence passed on the company by the National Assembly. The leaders said the company’s involvement in protecting oil and gas pipelines had coincided with improvements in security across some oil-producing communities, while also creating employment opportunities for young people. Commenting on the development, the President-General of the Isoko Development Union, Christopher Akpotu, commended the joint committee of the Senate and House of Representatives for dismissing petitions filed against the company. He described the decision as beneficial to the Niger Delta and urged stakeholders to focus on expanding opportunities in the oil and gas sector rather than internal disputes. “That is the right way to go,” he said. “There are many opportunities in the oil and gas sector. We should focus on how to derive more benefits rather than fighting over what has already been allocated.” Akpotu cautioned that prolonged disagreements among host communities could create opportunities for outsiders to benefit at their expense. “At the end of the day, if we continue fighting, we give room to those who have no stake in our communities to take what rightfully belongs to us,” he added. While acknowledging concerns about the distribution of benefits, he maintained that the surveillance contract had positively affected many communities. “The truth is that many communities and youths have benefited from the surveillance contract. It may not be evenly distributed, but the impact is evident,” he said. He also urged Tantita to broaden engagement with traditional rulers, youths and community leaders to promote fairness and strengthen long-term cooperation. Similarly, the President-General of the Ughelli Descendants Union, Sam Akpemegi, said security had improved since the company began operations, noting that both physical surveillance and intelligence-driven strategies had been deployed. “They have done very well and improved security since they began operations,” he said, adding that the company’s activities now extend across many communities. Akpemegi added that the company’s presence had benefited both traditional institutions and local residents, while calling for continued support for its operations. In the same vein, the Odiologbo of Ofagbe Kingdom, Ogaga Ikpoku, backed the National Assembly’s decision, describing it as timely and beneficial to peace and stability in the region. Stakeholders also argued that improved protection of oil assets has produced environmental and social benefits in parts of the Niger Delta. They noted that illegal refining and pipeline sabotage had previously polluted waterways, degraded farmlands and exposed communities to environmental and security risks, adding that sustained surveillance efforts against illegal refining sites and repeated pipeline breaches have supported ongoing environmental recovery. Eyesan said the absence of bids for the 13 frontier blocks was not unexpected. According to her, the assets presented during the 2025 commercial bid had previously been recovered from operators, while the commission would continue assessing other assets for possible inclusion in future licensing exercises. “The reason why we are in the market is because of the blocks that we have recovered from existing operators,” she said. Industry analysts also noted that the licensing round attracted notable investor interest in frontier basins. The others include Network E&P (2A40), Asharami (2A41), LexOil (2A42), BVOF (2A43), GupscoEnergy Limited (2A44 and 2A51), Saratoga (2A45), Volante (2A46), Concept-Reel Petroleum Services Limited (2A47 and 2A55), Clinton Oil Field (2A48 and 2A62), Nuway Oaklane Limited (2A49), Ramec (2A50), Italia (2A53), Blueridge E&P (2A54), Up Energies Limited (2A56), and AYM Shafa (2A57). There are also Blackrock Holdings Limited (2A58), Funtay Integrated Business Limited (2A59), Riparian Development and Production Limited (2A60), Nikstallis (2A61 and PPL 900), Stardeep Petroleum (PPL 2010), Dakoda & U Limited (PPL 308 and PPL 800), Southborne Oil and Gas Limited (PPL 902), Lanaka Petroleum (PPL 903), Highban Resources Limited (PPL 700) and Eyre Energy Limited (PPL 801). He described the decision as beneficial to the Niger Delta and urged stakeholders to focus on expanding opportunities in the oil and gas sector rather than internal disputes. “That is the right way to go,” he said. “There are many opportunities in the oil and gas sector. We should focus on how to derive more benefits rather than fighting over what has already been allocated.” Akpotu cautioned that prolonged disagreements among host communities could create opportunities for outsiders to benefit at their expense. “At the end of the day, if we continue fighting, we give room to those who have no stake in our communities to take what rightfully belongs to us,” he added. While acknowledging concerns about the distribution of benefits, he maintained that the surveillance contract had positively affected many communities. “The truth is that many communities and youths have benefited from the surveillance contract. It may not be evenly distributed, but the impact is evident,” he said. He also urged Tantita to broaden engagement with traditional rulers, youths and community leaders to promote fairness and strengthen long-term cooperation. Similarly, the President-General of the Ughelli Descendants Union, Sam Akpemegi, said security had improved since the company began operations, noting that both physical surveillance and intelligence-driven strategies had been deployed. “They have done very well and improved security since they began operations,” he said, adding that the company’s activities now extend across many communities. Akpemegi added that the company’s presence had benefited both traditional institutions and local residents, while calling for continued support for its operations. In the same vein, the Odiologbo of Ofagbe Kingdom, Ogaga Ikpoku, backed the National Assembly’s decision, describing it as timely and beneficial to peace and stability in the region. Stakeholders also argued that improved protection of oil assets has produced environmental and social benefits in parts of the Niger Delta. They noted that illegal refining and pipeline sabotage had previously polluted waterways, degraded farmlands and exposed communities to environmental and security risks, adding that sustained surveillance efforts against illegal refining sites and repeated pipeline breaches have supported ongoing environmental recovery. The companies that emerged as winners of the 2025 Licensing Round include SSonic Petroleum Limited (PPL 2A29), CFP Pipeline and Flowlines (2A30), Dutchford E&P Limited (2A32), Attabanson Global Company Limited (2A33 and PPL 901), Rosem Energy Limited (2A38), and Pivot-GIS Limited (2A39). The others include Network E&P (2A40), Asharami (2A41), LexOil (2A42), BVOF (2A43), GupscoEnergy Limited (2A44 and 2A51), Saratoga (2A45), Volante (2A46), Concept-Reel Petroleum Services Limited (2A47 and 2A55), Clinton Oil Field (2A48 and 2A62), Nuway Oaklane Limited (2A49), Ramec (2A50), Italia (2A53), Blueridge E&P (2A54), Up Energies Limited (2A56), and AYM Shafa (2A57). There are also Blackrock Holdings Limited (2A58), Funtay Integrated Business Limited (2A59), Riparian Development and Production Limited (2A60), Nikstallis (2A61 and PPL 900), Stardeep Petroleum (PPL 2010), Dakoda & U Limited (PPL 308 and PPL 800), Southborne Oil and Gas Limited (PPL 902), Lanaka Petroleum (PPL 903), Highban Resources Limited (PPL 700) and Eyre Energy Limited (PPL 801). Views from stakeholders Stakeholders have continued to emphasise the importance of pipeline protection to Nigeria’s oil production ambitions, arguing that safeguarding critical infrastructure remains essential to sustaining output, attracting investment and supporting broader economic growth. Related News Over 1,100 host community projects ongoing in Niger Delta, says NUPRC Oil block bidding: Inside Nigeria’s seven-hour transparency test REAN urges action to accelerate Nigeria’s clean energy transition In a published report, the President-General of the Niger Delta Progressive Alliance, Nse Udoh, argued that pipeline surveillance has helped national institutions move from reactive crisis management to more strategic planning and long-term decision-making. “It is important to clarify the role of pipeline surveillance within the wider energy landscape. Energy security encompasses the full value chain, from exploration and production to refining, distribution, pricing policy, and subsidy frameworks. Pipeline surveillance does not manage these domains,” he said. Udoh, however, maintained that pipeline surveillance plays a critical supporting role by protecting infrastructure that transports petroleum resources. “Its mandate is precise: safeguarding critical infrastructure that transports petroleum resources. Yet this single function has proven foundational. Without secure transportation channels, production targets falter, refining plans collapse, exports decline, and fiscal projections become unreliable,” he added. He further argued that asset protection underpins the efficient operation of the petroleum value chain. “Asset protection, in this context, is not a supporting activity. It is a precondition for economic order. In effect, the pipeline is the hinge on which the entire petroleum value chain turns. When that hinge is weak, every other link in the chain carries strain. When it is secure, the entire system gains coherence,” he wrote. According to him, sustained monitoring and rapid-response measures have contributed to fewer pipeline breaches and incidents of illegal tapping, while improvements in production have helped strengthen Nigeria’s position in the international oil market. Udoh also argued that greater certainty in crude oil transportation supports planning across the petroleum value chain. “Economic stability follows predictability. When crude flows are secure, refineries can plan feedstock intake with assurance. Export commitments can be met without fear of sudden shortfalls. Gas-to-power projects can operate without recurrent shutdown risks. “Investors can assess Nigeria’s petroleum sector with clearer risk profiles. Surveillance therefore does more than stop theft. It reintroduces reliability into national energy planning. And reliability is the bedrock upon which sustainable economic growth is built. “With predictable flows, national budgeting becomes more credible, infrastructure planning becomes more precise, and long-term contracts become easier to negotiate. Predictability is the silent currency of modern economies, and pipeline surveillance has begun restoring it,” he stated. He added that improved production accountability has supported export earnings, strengthened foreign exchange inflows and enhanced fiscal capacity, while helping national oil company operations become more efficient through lower losses and improved operational continuity. According to Udoh, more stable revenues also improve government planning by creating greater fiscal certainty and supporting investment in infrastructure, social services and economic diversification. Traditional rulers and community leaders in the Niger Delta have also called for continued support for Tantita Security Services Limited following a vote of confidence passed on the company by the National Assembly. The leaders said the company’s involvement in protecting oil and gas pipelines had coincided with improvements in security across some oil-producing communities, while also creating employment opportunities for young people. Commenting on the development, the President-General of the Isoko Development Union, Christopher Akpotu, commended the joint committee of the Senate and House of Representatives for dismissing petitions filed against the company. He described the decision as beneficial to the Niger Delta and urged stakeholders to focus on expanding opportunities in the oil and gas sector rather than internal disputes. “That is the right way to go,” he said. “There are many opportunities in the oil and gas sector. We should focus on how to derive more benefits rather than fighting over what has already been allocated.” Akpotu cautioned that prolonged disagreements among host communities could create opportunities for outsiders to benefit at their expense. “At the end of the day, if we continue fighting, we give room to those who have no stake in our communities to take what rightfully belongs to us,” he added. While acknowledging concerns about the distribution of benefits, he maintained that the surveillance contract had positively affected many communities. “The truth is that many communities and youths have benefited from the surveillance contract. It may not be evenly distributed, but the impact is evident,” he said. He also urged Tantita to broaden engagement with traditional rulers, youths and community leaders to promote fairness and strengthen long-term cooperation. Similarly, the President-General of the Ughelli Descendants Union, Sam Akpemegi, said security had improved since the company began operations, noting that both physical surveillance and intelligence-driven strategies had been deployed. “They have done very well and improved security since they began operations,” he said, adding that the company’s activities now extend across many communities. Akpemegi added that the company’s presence had benefited both traditional institutions and local residents, while calling for continued support for its operations. In the same vein, the Odiologbo of Ofagbe Kingdom, Ogaga Ikpoku, backed the National Assembly’s decision, describing it as timely and beneficial to peace and stability in the region. Stakeholders also argued that improved protection of oil assets has produced environmental and social benefits in parts of the Niger Delta. They noted that illegal refining and pipeline sabotage had previously polluted waterways, degraded farmlands and exposed communities to environmental and security risks, adding that sustained surveillance efforts against illegal refining sites and repeated pipeline breaches have supported ongoing environmental recovery. Traditional rulers and community leaders in the Niger Delta have also called for continued support for Tantita Security Services Limited following a vote of confidence passed on the company by the National Assembly. The leaders said the company’s involvement in protecting oil and gas pipelines had coincided with improvements in security across some oil-producing communities, while also creating employment opportunities for young people. Commenting on the development, the President-General of the Isoko Development Union, Christopher Akpotu, commended the joint committee of the Senate and House of Representatives for dismissing petitions filed against the company. He described the decision as beneficial to the Niger Delta and urged stakeholders to focus on expanding opportunities in the oil and gas sector rather than internal disputes. “That is the right way to go,” he said. “There are many opportunities in the oil and gas sector. We should focus on how to derive more benefits rather than fighting over what has already been allocated.” Akpotu cautioned that prolonged disagreements among host communities could create opportunities for outsiders to benefit at their expense. “At the end of the day, if we continue fighting, we give room to those who have no stake in our communities to take what rightfully belongs to us,” he added. While acknowledging concerns about the distribution of benefits, he maintained that the surveillance contract had positively affected many communities. “The truth is that many communities and youths have benefited from the surveillance contract. It may not be evenly distributed, but the impact is evident,” he said. He also urged Tantita to broaden engagement with traditional rulers, youths and community leaders to promote fairness and strengthen long-term cooperation. Similarly, the President-General of the Ughelli Descendants Union, Sam Akpemegi, said security had improved since the company began operations, noting that both physical surveillance and intelligence-driven strategies had been deployed. “They have done very well and improved security since they began operations,” he said, adding that the company’s activities now extend across many communities. Akpemegi added that the company’s presence had benefited both traditional institutions and local residents, while calling for continued support for its operations. In the same vein, the Odiologbo of Ofagbe Kingdom, Ogaga Ikpoku, backed the National Assembly’s decision, describing it as timely and beneficial to peace and stability in the region. Stakeholders also argued that improved protection of oil assets has produced environmental and social benefits in parts of the Niger Delta. They noted that illegal refining and pipeline sabotage had previously polluted waterways, degraded farmlands and exposed communities to environmental and security risks, adding that sustained surveillance efforts against illegal refining sites and repeated pipeline breaches have supported ongoing environmental recovery. In a published report, the President-General of the Niger Delta Progressive Alliance, Nse Udoh, argued that pipeline surveillance has helped national institutions move from reactive crisis management to more strategic planning and long-term decision-making. “It is important to clarify the role of pipeline surveillance within the wider energy landscape. Energy security encompasses the full value chain, from exploration and production to refining, distribution, pricing policy, and subsidy frameworks. Pipeline surveillance does not manage these domains,” he said. Udoh, however, maintained that pipeline surveillance plays a critical supporting role by protecting infrastructure that transports petroleum resources. “Its mandate is precise: safeguarding critical infrastructure that transports petroleum resources. Yet this single function has proven foundational. Without secure transportation channels, production targets falter, refining plans collapse, exports decline, and fiscal projections become unreliable,” he added. He further argued that asset protection underpins the efficient operation of the petroleum value chain. “Asset protection, in this context, is not a supporting activity. It is a precondition for economic order. In effect, the pipeline is the hinge on which the entire petroleum value chain turns. When that hinge is weak, every other link in the chain carries strain. When it is secure, the entire system gains coherence,” he wrote. According to him, sustained monitoring and rapid-response measures have contributed to fewer pipeline breaches and incidents of illegal tapping, while improvements in production have helped strengthen Nigeria’s position in the international oil market. Udoh also argued that greater certainty in crude oil transportation supports planning across the petroleum value chain. “Economic stability follows predictability. When crude flows are secure, refineries can plan feedstock intake with assurance. Export commitments can be met without fear of sudden shortfalls. Gas-to-power projects can operate without recurrent shutdown risks. “Investors can assess Nigeria’s petroleum sector with clearer risk profiles. Surveillance therefore does more than stop theft. It reintroduces reliability into national energy planning. And reliability is the bedrock upon which sustainable economic growth is built. “With predictable flows, national budgeting becomes more credible, infrastructure planning becomes more precise, and long-term contracts become easier to negotiate. Predictability is the silent currency of modern economies, and pipeline surveillance has begun restoring it,” he stated. He added that improved production accountability has supported export earnings, strengthened foreign exchange inflows and enhanced fiscal capacity, while helping national oil company operations become more efficient through lower losses and improved operational continuity. According to Udoh, more stable revenues also improve government planning by creating greater fiscal certainty and supporting investment in infrastructure, social services and economic diversification. He described the decision as beneficial to the Niger Delta and urged stakeholders to focus on expanding opportunities in the oil and gas sector rather than internal disputes. “That is the right way to go,” he said. “There are many opportunities in the oil and gas sector. We should focus on how to derive more benefits rather than fighting over what has already been allocated.” Akpotu cautioned that prolonged disagreements among host communities could create opportunities for outsiders to benefit at their expense. “At the end of the day, if we continue fighting, we give room to those who have no stake in our communities to take what rightfully belongs to us,” he added. While acknowledging concerns about the distribution of benefits, he maintained that the surveillance contract had positively affected many communities. “The truth is that many communities and youths have benefited from the surveillance contract. It may not be evenly distributed, but the impact is evident,” he said. He also urged Tantita to broaden engagement with traditional rulers, youths and community leaders to promote fairness and strengthen long-term cooperation. Similarly, the President-General of the Ughelli Descendants Union, Sam Akpemegi, said security had improved since the company began operations, noting that both physical surveillance and intelligence-driven strategies had been deployed. “They have done very well and improved security since they began operations,” he said, adding that the company’s activities now extend across many communities. Akpemegi added that the company’s presence had benefited both traditional institutions and local residents, while calling for continued support for its operations. In the same vein, the Odiologbo of Ofagbe Kingdom, Ogaga Ikpoku, backed the National Assembly’s decision, describing it as timely and beneficial to peace and stability in the region. Stakeholders also argued that improved protection of oil assets has produced environmental and social benefits in parts of the Niger Delta. They noted that illegal refining and pipeline sabotage had previously polluted waterways, degraded farmlands and exposed communities to environmental and security risks, adding that sustained surveillance efforts against illegal refining sites and repeated pipeline breaches have supported ongoing environmental recovery. “Economic stability follows predictability. When crude flows are secure, refineries can plan feedstock intake with assurance. Export commitments can be met without fear of sudden shortfalls. Gas-to-power projects can operate without recurrent shutdown risks. “Investors can assess Nigeria’s petroleum sector with clearer risk profiles. Surveillance therefore does more than stop theft. It reintroduces reliability into national energy planning. And reliability is the bedrock upon which sustainable economic growth is built. “With predictable flows, national budgeting becomes more credible, infrastructure planning becomes more precise, and long-term contracts become easier to negotiate. Predictability is the silent currency of modern economies, and pipeline surveillance has begun restoring it,” he stated. He added that improved production accountability has supported export earnings, strengthened foreign exchange inflows and enhanced fiscal capacity, while helping national oil company operations become more efficient through lower losses and improved operational continuity. According to Udoh, more stable revenues also improve government planning by creating greater fiscal certainty and supporting investment in infrastructure, social services and economic diversification. Traditional rulers and community leaders in the Niger Delta have also called for continued support for Tantita Security Services Limited following a vote of confidence passed on the company by the National Assembly. Stakeholders also argued that improved protection of oil assets has produced environmental and social benefits in parts of the Niger Delta. Title: South Korea's Naver jumps 10% on Nvidia's $1 billion investment plan
URL: https://www.channelnewsasia.com/business/south-koreas-naver-jumps-10-nvidias-1-billion-investment-plan-6279546
Time Published: 2026-07-26T23:04:39Z
Full Content:
East Asia FILE PHOTO: A general view of the Naver sign on its office building in Seongnam, South Korea, May 13, 2022. Picture taken May 13, 2022. REUTERS/ Heo Ran/File Photo SEOUL: Shares of South Korea's internet giant Naver jumped more than 10 per cent on Monday (Jul 27) after it said Nvidia would acquire US$1 billion of its new shares to finance a project to expand an AI data centre. The investment comes after the two firms and investment group Brookfield announced on Friday plans to expand Naver's AI data centre in South Korea with up to US$10 billion in funding. Naver said on Monday it will place 7.2 million new shares with Nvidia for 204,500 won each, or a 1 per cent discount to its closing price on Friday, as part of the agreement. Brookfield will provide up to US$9 billion in financing as the project's capital partner, Naver added. With the investment, Nvidia would become one of the biggest shareholders of Naver, owning a 4.5 per cent stake. The National Pension Service was the biggest shareholder of Naver with a 9.25 per cent stake, followed by BlackRock Fund Advisors with 6.12 per cent as of end-2025. The data centre will be built at Naver's GAK data centre in the city of Sejong in South Korea, using Nvidia's Vera Rubin and Blackwell chip platforms, and it will have a capacity of 200 megawatts by 2028. In June, Naver and Nvidia announced an agreement to jointly pursue the development of gigawatt-scale global AI infrastructure, aiming to tap demand for sovereign AI from the Asia-Pacific region, Europe and the Middle East. One gigawatt is about four times the maximum capacity of GAK Sejong, South Korea's largest hyperscale data centre operated by Naver. It represents a scale capable of accommodating hundreds of thousands of Nvidia's latest GPUs at once, Naver said. Title: BlackRock, Goldman Sachs, Fidelity, and others back the Clarity Act with $30 trillion in combined firepower
URL: https://cryptobriefing.com/blackrock-goldman-fidelity-back-clarity-act/
Time Published: 2026-07-26T19:03:48Z
Full Content:
Searching... The biggest names in traditional finance are lining up behind crypto market structure legislation ahead of Congress's August recess Share A coalition of financial heavyweights managing north of $30 trillion in assets has thrown its weight behind the Digital Asset Market Clarity Act, sending what might be the loudest signal yet that Wall Street wants crypto’s regulatory gray zone to end. BlackRock, Goldman Sachs, Fidelity, Charles Schwab, and Grayscale are among the firms endorsing H.R. 3633, a bill designed to draw clear jurisdictional lines between the SEC and CFTC over digital assets. The timing is deliberate. Congress is barreling toward its August recess, and supporters of the legislation are applying pressure to keep the bill moving through the Senate Banking Committee before lawmakers scatter for the summer. The Digital Asset Market Clarity Act, formally introduced on May 29, 2025, attempts to solve this by explicitly assigning oversight responsibilities between the two agencies. It also layers in anti-money laundering requirements, a concession that likely made the bill more palatable to regulators and lawmakers who’ve long worried about crypto’s use in illicit finance. Fidelity, which oversees approximately $7.1 trillion in assets, has been one of the most vocal supporters. The firm called on the Senate to pass the legislation, describing the framework as balanced and arguing it would bolster both investor confidence and US competitiveness in global digital asset markets. Goldman Sachs CEO David Solomon has also voiced support for the initiative, emphasizing the need for a structured market environment. The collective assets under management of the firms backing this bill exceed $30 trillion, with some estimates placing the combined figure closer to $50 trillion depending on how you count. Most of these firms have already dipped their toes into crypto. BlackRock launched its spot Bitcoin ETF in early 2024 and watched it become one of the most successful ETF launches in history. Fidelity has offered crypto custody and trading for years. But their engagement has been constrained, deliberately limited to the safest corners of the market because the regulatory picture has been so murky. The Senate Banking Committee’s movement on the bill suggests genuine legislative momentum. The FIT21 bill passed the House in 2024 with broad support and then languished in the Senate. Supporters of H.R. 3633 are clearly trying to avoid a repeat. Title: Former minister for children calls for pause on planned development at Bessborough
URL: https://www.irishtimes.com/ireland/2026/07/26/former-minister-for-children-calls-for-pause-on-planned-development-at-bessborough/
Time Published: 2026-07-26T18:23:43Z
Full Content:
Former minister for children Katherine Zappone has called for a pause on a controversial planned development at the site of a former mother and baby home. Earlier this year Estuary View Enterprises was granted planning permission by Cork City Council to build 140 apartments on the grounds of Bessborough in Blackrock on the southside of Cork city. Bessborough Mother and Baby Home Support Group and Cllr Peter Horgan of Labour lodged appeals to An Coimisiún Pleanála. On July 9th the commission upheld permission for the building of 108 apartments on the site. This caused dismay among survivors of the home who are vehemently opposed to the development. The Commission of Investigation into Mother and Baby Homes estimated that 923 babies died at the home between the 1920s and 1990s. The burial place of more than 859 children remains unknown. In an interview with RTÉ’s This Week programme Zappone said significant efforts should be made to determine the scale of burial at Bessborough. “I think this can and should be done now before development. I think there should be a pause now in relation to the development. [ Former minister Zappone raised concerns about building apartments on Bessborough siteOpens in new window ] “The careful, honest and moral thing to do is to look properly before we build. “Why would the State not want to do something about this? “We know 859 [children] and 19 mothers died at Bessborough and they have no known grave.” The former minister believes a geophysical survey ought to be carried out at Bessborough. “We need to have a similar approach to Bessborough as we did in Tuam, it needs to be taken one step at a time and to determine how to do that. “The primary concerns, as I hear it, and as our special advocate Patricia Carey talks about, they [the victims and survivors] have the right to know where their loved ones are buried and that’s what I am arguing for.” [ State urged to buy former Bessborough mother and baby home lands to create memorial parkOpens in new window ] Zappone said what was done in Tuam “wasn’t done in Bessborough”. She indicated that when she was minister for children she received the burial reports from the commission of investigation. “In that report they identified that there were modest efforts to ascertain the location and the scale of the burial at Bessborough. “Forensic archaeologists basically assessed the visible landscape but determined that there were no visible features that indicated that there was something or that human remains may be buried underneath.” Zappone insisted this wasn’t “sufficient” nor was it comparable to what occurred at Tuam. Meanwhile, in a letter of appeal to the commission earlier this year Zappone expressed her belief in the “human dignity” of the mothers and children associated with Bessborough whose burial places are not known. However, in its decision to uphold planning permission the commission stated there was no evidence of unrecorded burials in the area where the development is proposed. Conditions attached to the planning permission include forensic archaeological monitoring during construction work and the appointment of a liaison officer to engage with survivor groups. Title: You Langer: Colette Sheridan on a wonderful new book of Cork’s slang words
URL: https://www.irishtimes.com/opinion/an-irish-diary/2026/07/26/you-langer-colette-sheridan-on-a-wonderful-new-book-of-corks-slang-words/
Time Published: 2026-07-26T18:00:00Z
Full Content:
A self-described blow-in from Mayo who has only been living in Cork for more than 50 years, Des MacHale, author of joke books and emeritus professor of mathematics at University College Cork, recently had his latest book launched at the Kerryman’s bar, Cork. Langers & Lashes: A Compendium of Cork Slang claims to be the most comprehensive collection of Cork slang published. The title describes utter fools or arrogant types and attractive women. (“Langer” is also a Cork word for the male appendage and its plural form refers to someone wasted from drink.) Singer Tim O’Riordan belted out verses of his irreverent ballad, The Langer Song, at the launch. It was followed by a couple of words from MacHale’s son Dominic – an actor best known for playing Garda Sergeant Healy in the TV series The Young Offenders. (Dominic MacHale and fellow actor Shane Casey, who plays Billy Murphy in the Cork northside-based comedy, were taking time off from watching the World Cup for their humorous take on the games for RTÉ 2.) As the author’s son said, it takes an outsider to identify the slang that trips off the tongues of the natives. The book is certainly comprehensive with the inclusion of some slang that this writer never heard of despite being a Corkonian – albeit from the southside. That north/south dichotomy is important in Cork. “Norries” living north of the river Lee are generally seen as salt of the earth (think Roy Keane), unlike the “Sorries” who have notions, such as the fictional social media “Reggie” character living in a mansion on the salubrious Blackrock Road. (As it happens, Des MacHale is a resident of Blackrock.) This suburb would not be a hotbed of Cork slang. Which is why MacHale is indebted to members of the Frank O’Connor Book Club in Mayfield and the Blackpool Library Creative Writing Circle on the north side for their valuable contributions to the book. MacHale writes that most of the slang words in his book are words he has heard used in Cork over the years or words that have been reported to him as being in use. Apart from its religious connotation, I’ve never heard of the other meaning of “immaculate conception”, which refers to someone who develops a drink problem without ever standing a round. This phrase was imparted to MacHale by the late humorist, Niall Tóibín. Some of the slang in the book is “potentially offensive”, but rather than being censorious the dictionary preserves, in the interests of their historical contexts, words that embody prejudices that society now rejects. Crude anatomical and sexual references are included in the book to reflect the vernacular of old. Women come in for a disproportionate amount of derogatory terms, judging their behaviour, appearance and sexuality, compared to men. A difficult woman is “a wagon” while a good-looking female is a “fine half” or a “flah”. The latter word is also used as a verb for sexual congress. These terms are familiar to Corkonians, but how often is a word such as “balubas” used? To be balubas is to be seriously drunk. The term originated when Cork soldiers serving in the United Nations peacekeeping forces in the Democratic Republic of Congo (1960-1964) “inappropriately adopted the name of the Baluba people as slang. While still in use, using an African people’s name in this derogatory way reflects colonial-era attitudes that are recognised as offensive today”. Hardly offensive, though, except to particularly uptight parents, is the term “electrocution lessons” to help a child modify their accent and speech patterns. As a kid, I was sent to elocution classes (we never used the word electrocution in that jokey way) to try to nullify my burgeoning Cork accent. Southside mothers had antennae for picking up that sing/song way of speaking that wouldn’t help one’s career prospects on South Mall. Coming home from playing on the streets, an unsuspecting kid might ask for a glass of raza (raspberry cordial). The only response was to call in reinforcements, which involved packing him/her off to elocution sessions. Do people still send their kids to elocution? Or would that be considered too elitist these days? Still, the slang phrase “he spoke very well dressed” describes someone with a “superior accent or method of speaking”. I haven’t come across it, but it is in keeping with the Cork sense of humour, mixing and matching unlikely concepts so they make a certain kind of sense. In a note from the publisher, Mercier, the book is described as intending to be a scholarly reference on the development of Cork vernacular and not as a guide for contemporary usage. But we still hang on to such gems as “getting a fifty”, which sums up being ghosted when only 50 per cent turns up for a date. Cork slang is, of course, superior to that of any other slang in the country. Admittedly, we natives are guilty of Cork exceptionalism – which perhaps merits a book all of its own. © 2026 The Irish Times DAC
